PydanticAI sends tool results as **compact JSON**, so TOON's real saving for us is **27–40%** on list-shaped results.

#### BUG-7 fix proposal (based on the measurements)
**1. Calibrated, two-kind estimator** (`memory/tokens.py`):
- **Prose:** 2.4 chars/token. The lowest measured was 2.59, so this leaves ~8% headroom.
- **JSON** (tool schemas, tool results): 1.9 chars/token. The lowest measured was 2.03, so ~7% headroom.
- **Hidden tool-use overhead:** 350 tokens added to every request that offers tools (measured ~320).

The constants record the model, the date and how they were measured. `scripts/calibrate_tokens.py`, run with `make calibrate` (needs a key), re-measures and prints new ratios, and a human updates the constants. The estimate stays **deterministic and offline**.

**2. Repro test with recorded measurements:** `tests/data/token_calibration.json` stores the **exact texts** measured today (the tool schemas, the system prompt, a seeded prose sample) and their real counts. The test checks our estimate ≥ the real count for each, fully offline. It fails today: 2,012 vs 2,712+320, 465 vs 542, 669 vs 904.

**3. A consequence to decide: the budget becomes honest, and tight.** In real tokens the fixed part is tool schemas + overhead + system ≈ **3,600**. Out of 6,000, after memories (600) and working room (1,500), only ~300 would be left for this session's history. Options:
- **Raise the default budget to 8,000** (my recommendation). The assignment asks for a *fixed* cap, not a particular number, and 8,000 leaves about 2,300 tokens for history.
- Keep 6,000 and reclaim room by slimming the tool definitions first. That's a follow-up and doesn't need to block this fix.

**4. `tiktoken` deferred.** Nobody uses an OpenAI model here yet. Unknown models get the same conservative ratios, and the trace's calibration field will show drift if anyone switches. I'll note this in DECISIONS.

**TOON:** worth adopting, but as its own step after the bugs. Use it for the flat lists (`list_memories`, `find_my_bookings`), and keep JSON for search options, whose nested slot lists the model must read exactly. It'll get a test that checks the model is still given every id and slot it needs.

**Claude**

Agreed. The budget should come from what the agent actually needs plus evidence about what's good for the model, not from what we happen to be spending. Two parts: (1) what research and real systems say about context size, and (2) measuring our actual working set, so the number can be derived rather than picked.

- `WebSearch` Chroma context rot study results performance drop at what input length short contexts 1000 tokens
- `WebSearch` Anthropic context editing clear_tool_uses default trigger input_tokens 100000 keep tool uses
- `WebSearch` tau-bench tool-agent-user benchmark average tokens per task conversation length customer service agent
- `WebSearch` OpenAI function calling best practices number of tools fewer than 20 accuracy tool definitions tokens
- `Bash` Read search_and_compare and find_my_bookings return shapes
- `Write` <scratchpad>/measure_working_set.
- `Bash` Measure real token sizes of tool results and chat turns

I now have both halves: what the evidence says, and what our agent actually needs, measured.

#### 1. What research and real systems say
- **No source gives a "right" budget for a task agent.** The consensus is a *principle*: "the smallest set of high-signal tokens" (Anthropic).
- **Smaller is better for accuracy.** Chroma found all 18 frontier models degrade as input grows, starting "well before the window is full", and irrelevant "distractor" content makes it worse. No source puts a number on where degradation starts.
- **Where real systems draw lines:**
  - Anthropic's context-editing feature starts clearing old tool results at **100,000** input tokens by default (examples use 30,000).
  - For tools, OpenAI says "keep the number of initially available functions small"; Together AI suggests fewer than 20.
- **What follows:** at 6–8k tokens we're far below anywhere degradation has been shown to matter. The real risk at this size isn't context rot. It's **starving the conversation history**. So the budget should be the **smallest that fits our worst realistic turn**.

#### 2. What our agent actually needs (real tokens, measured just now)

| Component | Real tokens | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Tool schemas (16 tools) + hidden tool-use prompt | **~3,030** | `count_tokens` |
| System prompt | 542 | `count_tokens` |
| State (errand, pending card, addresses) | ~100–200 | from traces × measured ratio |
| **Fixed per request** | **~3,700** | |
| Memories (cap) | ≤ 600 | our setting |
| **Working room**, worst realistic run (reschedule: `find_my_bookings` 1,002 + `available_slots` 340 + calls) | **~1,500** | measured tool results |
| (booking run: requirements 104 + search 525 + proposal 106 + calls) | (~900) | measured |
| **History**, your live chat: 21 messages, avg 86, max 400 tokens; a 10-exchange errand session | **~1,700** | your messages in the dev DB |
| **Total need** | **≈ 7,500** | |

#### 3. What this means
- **6,000 is too small, but not because we spent more.** The fixed part alone (~3,700) eats 60% of it, leaving ~300 for history after memories and working room.
- **The derived need is ~7,500.** 8,000 is that plus about 6% headroom. Not blind, but not generous either.
- **There's one outlier that doesn't fit at all:** `list_memories` returns **2,326** tokens, more than the whole working room. That confirms the follow-up: trimming it to booking-relevant facts plus a note count is necessary for the budget, not just nice to have.
- **We can lower the need later.** Tool schemas are the largest fixed cost (2,712). Trimming descriptions and overlapping parameters, per the "minimal tool set" advice, could plausibly save 500–800, bringing the need near **7,000**.

#### Recommendation
1. **BUG-7:** calibrate the estimator (prose 2.4, JSON 1.9, +350 tool overhead), using the recorded-measurement test.
2. **Set the budget to 7,500**, with its derivation (the table above) recorded in DECISIONS.
3. **Trim `list_memories`** right after, since its current size can't fit the working room.
4. Later, slim the tool schemas and re-derive; the budget may drop to ~7,000.

Does deriving it this way, and landing at 7,500, make sense to you?

**You** · 10:01 UTC

yeah what I want is what usually the agentic chat spent in terms of conversation

**Claude**

Got it: you want a benchmark from the outside world. How many turns and tokens do real agentic and chat conversations typically use? Here's what's published:

- `WebSearch` LMSYS-Chat-1M dataset statistics average turns per conversation average tokens per prompt response
- `WebSearch` WildChat dataset statistics average number of turns tokens per conversation
- `WebSearch` tau-bench retail airline average number of turns tool calls per task tokens cost per task
- `WebSearch` customer support AI agent average tokens per conversation cost per resolution input tokens per turn

The τ²-bench leaderboard publishes per-task token averages for a real tool-using customer-service agent, the closest public analogue to ours. I'll read it directly:

- `WebFetch` https://openrouter.ai/benchmarks/tau2-bench-airline

Here's what's published about real conversation sizes, set against ours.

#### How long real conversations are

| Source | What it measures | Turns per conversation | Tokens |
|---|---|---|---|
| **LMSYS-Chat-1M** (1M real chats, 25 models) | general chat | **2.0** avg | user prompt **~70**, reply **~215** per turn |
| **WildChat** (1M real ChatGPT logs) | general chat | **2.5** avg; ~41% multi-turn | user **~296**, reply **~441** per turn |
| **τ-bench** (customer-service agents with tools, closest to ours) | agentic task | retail **4–7**, airline **6–10** turns; **3–9 tool calls**; capped at 30 actions | (turn counts from a secondary source, unverified) |
| **τ²-bench airline**, Claude Sonnet 5.5 (our model) | agentic task | — | **$0.16 per task**, 6.25k output tokens. At $2 / $10 per million, that implies **~49k input tokens across the whole task** |
| Vendor cost models for support agents | per model call | ~4 calls per conversation | **600–1,500 input** per call (rough, vendor-modelled) |

The token counts in the first two rows use Llama-2's tokenizer, so they're indicative only. Claude produces more tokens for the same text.

#### How we compare (real Claude tokens)

| | Typical out there | Ours |
|---|---|---|
| Turns in an errand conversation | 4–10 (τ-bench) | your session: ~10 messages before booking anything |
| Size of a chat message | ~70–440 per turn | **avg 86, max 400** (your live chat) |
| **History for one task** | 4–10 turns × ~150–600 ≈ **0.6k–6k** | our need: **~1.7k** (10 exchanges) |
| Input per model call | 600–1,500 (vendor estimate) up to ~3–5k (derived from τ²) | **3.7k–7.5k** |
| Cost per task | τ² on Sonnet 5.5: **$0.16** | your plumber turn: $0.045; a full booking is roughly $0.15–0.30 |

#### What this tells us
1. **Our conversation part is normal.** About 1.7k tokens for a 10-exchange errand sits in the middle of what real agent tasks use. History isn't where we're heavy.
2. **Our cost per task is in line with a published agent benchmark** on the same model (τ²: $0.16).
3. **Our per-call input runs high, and the cause is the fixed part:** 16 tool schemas plus the hidden tool prompt (~3,000) and the system prompt (~540) are **~3,700 tokens sent on every call**, before any conversation. The vendor estimates of 600–1,500 per call imply much leaner setups.

**The conclusion changes the plan slightly.** A 7,500 budget is consistent with the outside data, but it's sized around our **fixed overhead**, not our conversations. So the better order is:
1. **BUG-7:** calibrate the estimator so the cap is honest.
2. **Slim the fixed part:** trim the tool schemas and `list_memories`, the two big outliers.
3. **Then derive the budget from the slimmed numbers.** History needs ~1.7k, memories 600 and working room ~1,500. If the fixed part drops to ~2.5–3k, the budget lands around **6,000–6,500**, so the original 6,000 might turn out right once we're lean.

For now, BUG-7 would set the budget to the honest current need (7,500) so nothing breaks, and step 3 revisits it with real numbers. Does that order work for you?

#### Next: BUG-8 (the model doesn't know what code showed you)
There are two ways to tell the model, and I recommend the first:

| Option | How | Trade-off |
|---|---|---|
| **A. A note in the saved reply** (recommended) | When code shows suggestions, the saved assistant message gets a code-written line: *"[The app showed the user 2 suggestions to review.]"* Next turn, the model sees it in its own history. | Truthful record of what happened in the conversation; the count only, never the values; ~15 tokens. A web transcript would show the note too, which is accurate. |
| B. A STATE line | STATE says "N suggestions await review" | Tells the model they *exist*, but not whether they were already *shown*, which was the actual mistake. Knowing that would need a new "shown" record in the database. |

**Repro:** turn 1, the model calls `show_suggestions`. Turn 2: what the model receives must say the suggestions were shown, and must never contain "Sparks" or "CoolBreeze".

Shall I go ahead with A?

**You** · 10:13 UTC

also can you make a graceful exit on ctrl+c please (if it's not too much changes)

**Claude**

That's a small change. Ctrl+C **at the prompt** already exits quietly. What isn't graceful is Ctrl+C **while a turn is running**: it escapes `asyncio.run` as a traceback. The fix is one `except KeyboardInterrupt` in `main()` with a clear message.

Interrupting mid-turn is also safe for bookings. If a booking was in flight, the reconcile pass checks it with the provider at the next start, so the message can say exactly that. Test and code go in one commit, as for any feature:

- `Bash` Branch and view main()'s run call and a main() test
- `Edit` ~/Documents/temp/errand-agent/backend/tests/unit/test_chat_cli.py
- `Bash` Confirm the new test fails first

- `Edit` ~/Documents/temp/errand-agent/backend/src/errand_agent/api/chat.py
- `Edit` ~/Documents/temp/errand-agent/backend/src/errand_agent/api/chat.py

Ctrl+C now exits cleanly even in the middle of a turn. It's merged into `feat/agent` (`bb2746d`), all 646 tests pass, and lint is clean.

- **At the prompt:** it already left quietly, so nothing changed there.
- **During a turn:** instead of a traceback, you now see *"Interrupted. Anything that was in progress is checked with the provider the next time you start, so nothing is booked twice."*

That message is true because of the startup reconcile pass. If you interrupt while a booking request is in flight, the next start asks the provider what actually happened before doing anything else.

It was a small change: one `except` in `main()`, plus a test that simulates the interrupt. Before the fix, the simulated Ctrl+C stopped pytest itself, which is the same traceback you'd have seen.

Next up is step 1 from the plan: slim `list_memories` and the tool schemas, then re-derive the budget. Shall I go ahead?

**You** · 10:15 UTC

run make test a lot of tests are failing

**Claude**

- `Bash` Run make test and show the results

On my side, `make test` passes: **646 passed, 0 failed**, on `feat/agent` with a clean working tree. So the difference is in how or where it runs for you. Three likely causes:

1. **Environment variables in your shell.** If `ANTHROPIC_API_KEY`, `ERRAND_MODEL`, `ERRAND_DATA_DIR` or similar are **exported** in your terminal, pydantic-settings reads them even in tests that skip `.env`. Tests like "without a key it says what to set" or "default models are announced" would then fail. This is my top suspect: the run above uses my shell, not yours.
2. **A different checkout state:** another branch, or uncommitted local changes.
3. **A stale virtualenv.** If dependencies changed and `uv sync` hasn't run, imports or versions can differ. That would usually break many tests at once, which matches "a lot".

To narrow it down, could you run these and paste the output? The first prints only variable **names**, never values or keys:
```
! env | grep -E '^(ERRAND_|ANTHROPIC|OPENAI|GOOGLE|PYDANTIC)' | cut -d= -f1
! git status --short && git branch --show-current
! make test 2>&1 | grep -E "^FAILED|^ERROR|passed|failed" | head -30
```
The list of failing test names will tell us which cause it is. If they're all config or chat tests, it's #1. If they're failing at collection or import, it's #3.

**Claude**

The cause is in your output: **`OSError: [Errno 24] Too many open files`**. Every failure is either that or its side effect, SQLite's "unable to open database file". So it isn't a logic problem. The test suite is running out of **file descriptors**. Let me check why it passes for me but not for you:

- `Bash` Compare open-file limits and reproduce with macOS default
- `Bash` Measure file descriptors left open by two test files
- `Bash` Measure open descriptors without forcing garbage collection
- `Write` <scratchpad>/fdpeak.py
- `Bash` Measure peak open descriptors per test file
- `Bash` Check whether a subset fails under low file limits

A subset doesn't reproduce it, which shows how the leak works. Unclosed connections only pile up **across the whole session**: our test objects reference each other in cycles (World → stores → connection), so Python frees them only when its occasional full garbage collection runs. The full suite peaks at ~300 descriptors before that happens.

#### Why it fails for you and not for me
- **The cause is ours:** the tests never explicitly **close** their SQLite connections. Each connection in WAL mode holds up to 3 files (`.db`, `-wal`, `-shm`), and test helpers like `World.mock_exec` / `mock_count` open a **new** connection on every call.
- **Why it passed here:** my shell allows 1,048,576 open files, while macOS defaults to **256**. Peak usage is ~300, so it only fails on a normal Mac. My gate couldn't see it. Under `ulimit -n 256` I reproduce your exact failures.
- **The app itself is fine.** The running app closes its connections (`Services.close()`). This is a test-suite defect, but it's real, committed, and breaks `make test` for anyone on default settings. So it's **BUG-9**.

**Workaround right now:** `ulimit -n 4096 && make test` (only for that terminal session).

#### Proposed fix
- **Repro:** two tests in order. The first opens a connection; the second checks it was closed when the first test ended. Today it's still open, so it's marked `xfail(strict)`. It's fast and deterministic, and doesn't depend on your file limit or GC timing.
- **Fix:**
  1. A `conftest.py` fixture that tracks every SQLite connection a test opens and **closes them all** when the test ends. That's about 10 lines covering all 646 tests, instead of editing ~60 places that build a `World`.
  2. `mock_exec` / `mock_count` close their connection immediately, since they shouldn't open one per call and leave it.
- **Check:** with the fix, the full suite must pass under `ulimit -n 256`, and I'll show the peak count before and after.

BUG-9 is fixed and merged into `feat/agent` (`da178a9`): logged `fdf3f8d`, repro `dda6be5`, fix `7a57433`, close-out `5aedec3`. All 648 tests pass, and lint is clean.

**What was wrong:** the tests never closed their SQLite connections. Each one holds up to 3 open files, and they were only freed when Python got around to a full garbage collection. Across the suite that peaked at ~300 open files, over macOS's default limit of 256. My shell's limit is about a million, so my checks never saw it.

**The fix:**
- A shared test fixture now closes every database connection a test opened, as soon as that test ends.
  - Connections the app opens in its own thread during API tests are left alone. SQLite won't let another thread touch them, and the app's shutdown already closes them.
- The two test helpers that opened a fresh connection on every call now close it straight away.
- **Peak open files dropped from ~302 to 23.** The suite passes under the default limit of 256 and even under 64.

**The gate can't miss this again:** `make test` now runs under `ulimit -n 256`. A future leak fails for me too, not just on a default Mac.

I'd do **items 1–3 first, then test the chat once**.

**Why that order:**
- **The chat test mainly checks what offline tests can't:** real token counts against our calibrated estimate, and how the real model behaves. Items 1 and 2 change exactly those things (what `list_memories` returns, the tool definitions, the budget). A chat test now would mostly need repeating afterwards, and each run costs real tokens.
- **The bug fixes are already covered by offline tests.** Every one has a regression test, so a live run isn't what proves them.
- **Item 3 is small** and changes wording you'd notice in the chat, so it's better done before you look.

**The case for testing now** is isolation: if something looks wrong later, we'd know whether the bug fixes or the slimming caused it. That risk is low here. The slimming changes data shapes and descriptions, not the approval, booking or memory logic, and the traces show exactly what each turn sent.

**So the order would be:**
1. Slim `list_memories` (booking-relevant facts + "and 30 notes").
2. Trim the tool schemas, re-measure with `make calibrate`, and re-derive the budget.
3. Fix the `/confirm` hint and the "started looking" wording.
4. **You run `make chat`.** One session validates everything, using the checklist above.
5. TOON experiment → `make smoke` → merge Phase 5.

Item 1 is done (`4a4187e`). On to **item 2: trimming the tool schemas**, our largest fixed cost. First, where the tokens actually go, tool by tool:

- `Bash` Measure each tool definition's token cost
- `Bash` Print the two largest tool parameter schemas

Now I know where the bloat comes from, so here's a proposal before changing anything.

#### Where the 2,588 tokens of tool definitions go
1. **How optional parameters are written (~40 tokens each).** Python's `area: str | None = None` turns into a verbose JSON Schema: `"anyOf": [{"type": "string"}, {"type": "null"}], "default": null`. `update_requirements` has 10 optional parameters (415 tokens), and `remember`, `correct_memory`, `add_address`, `update_profile` and `available_slots` repeat the pattern. A parameter that is simply *not required* already tells the model it can leave it out, so the `null` branch only adds tokens.
2. **Long descriptions:** `list_memories` (154, my own doing earlier), `show_suggestions` (88), `decline_pending` (87), `search_and_compare` (68) and `get_reviews_summary` (66). Some of that wording repeats what the system prompt already says.
3. Tools with no parameters carry about 28 tokens of boilerplate each, a small cost that isn't worth fighting.

#### Proposal
**A. Simpler schemas for optional parameters**, the big win, with no change to behaviour:
- PydanticAI lets a tool use a custom schema generator. Ours would write `X | None = None` as just `X` (not required). Validation stays exactly the same, so a `null` from the model is still accepted.
- One small class, applied to every tool in `orchestrator_tools()`.
- **Expected savings: ~400–500 tokens** (`update_requirements` alone: ~415 → ~200).

**B. Tighter descriptions** for the five longest, without dropping any rule the model needs:
- Anything the system prompt already states moves out of the description.
- **Expected savings: ~150–250 tokens.**

**C. No merging or removing of tools.** Fewer tools would save more, but it changes what the model can do and needs behaviour testing. It's not worth the risk right now.

**Guard rails:**
- A test pins **every tool's parameter names, types and required flags** before and after, so slimming can't silently change the contract.
- A test caps the total: tool definitions under ~2,000 estimated tokens, so they can't creep back up.
- Re-run `make calibrate` (free) on the new schemas, then **re-derive the budget** from the measured table. Probably 7,500 → ~6,800.

That totals roughly **600–700 fewer tokens on every request** (~25% of the fixed tool cost). Shall I go ahead with A and B?

**Why it's worth anything:** tool definitions are a **fixed cost on every request**, about 3,000 of our 7,500 budget, before any conversation.

**What ~600–700 fewer tokens per request buys:**

| Benefit | Size |
|---|---|
| **More room for the conversation** within the same budget | +~650 tokens of history, roughly 7 more chat messages per request |
| **Or a smaller budget** for the same conversation | 7,500 → ~6,800 |
| **Cost** | ~$0.0013 per request on Sonnet; a booking (~10–25 requests) saves ~$0.01–0.03 |
| **Accuracy** | small: less context means less for the model to wade through (the "context rot" research) |

**Honest assessment:** it's a modest gain, not a fix. Nothing is broken today. The biggest win, `list_memories` (about 2,700 tokens saved per call), is already done. This is about 25% off the remaining fixed cost, with low risk thanks to the contract test.

I'd still do A, since it's one small class and the biggest part of the saving, and keep B light. Skipping both is reasonable too if you'd rather move on to the chat test. Which do you prefer?

So providers, slots, prices, reviews and bookings all come from our mock world (`mocks/data/providers.json` plus seeded randomness). Only the **LLM is real**, which the assignment also requires: "use a real LLM to understand requests and drive tool calls".

- **Budget too low → honest "no results"**, then it offered to relax the budget and used your saved ₹1,500 limit instead of asking again.

- **Fix:** about 3 lines. The chat tracks whether a turn is running and picks the message accordingly: "Bye." at the prompt, the reconcile note mid-turn.

#### Next: TOON — decision point
Before building anything, here's what the experiment would involve and what it would buy:

**What it saves (measured on our data, real tokens):**

| Result | compact JSON | TOON | saving | how often |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `find_my_bookings` (11 bookings) | ~1,160 | ~850 | ~310 | only when the model looks up bookings |
| `list_memories` (now slim) | ~650 | ~450 | ~200 | only when it lists memories |
| search options | — | — | none | nested slot lists: TOON's weak spot, we'd keep JSON |

So it's **~200–300 tokens, only on turns that call those two tools**. Unlike the tool-definition trimming, which saves on *every* request.

**What it costs and risks:**
- **Accuracy on Claude is unproven.** The independent test found TOON, JSON and YAML about equal on Claude Haiku 4.5, and TOON *worst* on nested data. These results carry booking ids the model must copy exactly into `start_cancel` / `start_reschedule`. A mis-read id is caught by validation, but it costs a retry.
- **Our estimator would need re-calibrating.** TOON is plain text, so the guard would count it at the prose ratio. TOON is denser than prose, so we could **undercount**, which reopens BUG-7 unless we calibrate a TOON sample too.
- An encoder (~30 lines) with escaping edge cases, and traces become less structured to read.
- Proving "the model still gets every id" offline only shows the *text* contains them, not that the model reads them correctly. That needs live evals.

**My recommendation: don't adopt TOON now.** Record it in D3 as *evaluated and deferred*, with these measurements. The saving is small and occasional, and it risks two things we just fixed (honest token counts, exact ids). If the eval phase later shows tool results crowding the budget, it's the first thing to revisit.

Shall I record it as deferred and move on to `make smoke`, or would you rather run the experiment anyway?

#### TOON experiment plan (on a throwaway branch)
`experiment/toon`, cut from `main`. If it doesn't pay off, we delete the branch and nothing else changes.

**What gets built:**
1. **A small encoder** (`agent/toon.py`). It turns a flat, uniform list into TOON, and falls back to compact JSON for anything nested or irregular.
2. **It's applied only to the two flat lists:** `find_my_bookings` and `list_memories`. Search options stay JSON, because their nested slot lists are TOON's weak spot.
3. **Token counting stays honest.** The context guard will count *any* tool result as dense data, not prose. `make calibrate` gets TOON samples, so the never-undercount test covers them too.
4. **An offline test** checks every id, slot and status survives the encoding.

**How we decide, both conditions required:**
- **Savings:** at least 25% fewer real tokens on those results, measured with `count_tokens` (free).
- **Accuracy:** a tiny live eval. The model gets the user's bookings as JSON vs TOON and is asked "cancel my AC servicing", 5 runs each. TOON must pick the correct booking id every time JSON does. That's ~10 small model calls, roughly **$0.10**.

If both hold, we keep it and merge with a D3 note. If either fails, we record the result in D3 ("evaluated, not adopted", with numbers) on `main` and delete the branch.

TOON paid off, so it's adopted, merged into `main` and pushed (`2385f50`). All 659 tests pass and lint is clean. The experiment's commits stay visible in the history.

**Results** (the bar was at least 25% fewer tokens and no loss of accuracy):

| | JSON | TOON |
|---|---|---|
| Bookings list (real tokens) | 1,162 | **748 (−36%)** |
| Live eval: correct id (claude-sonnet-5-5, 10 questions) | 9/10 | **10/10** |
| Input tokens per question | ~1,058 | **~725 (−31%)** |

JSON's one miss answered with a list instead of the id, so it wasn't really a reading error. The eval is small and the lookups simple: enough to rule out a reading penalty, not to claim TOON reads better. That caveat is recorded in D3. The live eval cost about $0.05.

**A real problem the experiment caught:** TOON is *denser* than JSON (1.75 vs 2.04 chars per token). Counted with the JSON ratio, the context budget would have been undercounted, the same failure as BUG-7. The calibration test flagged it immediately, so TOON results now get their own ratio (1.6).

**What changed:**
- `find_my_bookings` and `list_memories` return TOON tables. Search results stay JSON, because their nested slot lists are where TOON does worst.
- The live agent uses TOON. Unit tests that build the agent's dependencies directly stay on JSON, which keeps their assertions readable.
- DECISIONS D3, ARCHITECTURE and BUGS.md record the method, the numbers and the caveats.

- FastAPI routes with a live event stream
- chat and approval-card panes, a live trace pane, and a memory panel with Confirm/Reject buttons
- the per-session lock that was deferred earlier

Decided: **chat plus a tabbed side panel** (Trace · Memory · Bookings · Demo), a **dev-only demo panel**, and **one-line trace events that expand on click**. Here's the Phase 6 proposal, with no code yet.

#### Phase 6 plan: API and web UI

##### Backend API (FastAPI, all routes under `/api`)
| Endpoint | Purpose |
|---|---|
| `POST /sessions` | start a session for the demo user |
| `GET /sessions/{id}/messages` | conversation history, so a page reload shows the chat. The code-written "[The app showed…]" note is stripped for display. |
| `POST /sessions/{id}/messages` `{text}` | run one turn and return the reply: message, outcome, approval card, booking, suggestions, run id |
| `POST /approvals/{id}/decision` `{approve, fingerprint}` | the **button path**: grant (`via="button"`) or decline in code, then execute, exactly like typed yes/no |
| `GET /memories` · `POST /memories/{id}/confirm`·`/reject` · `PATCH /memories/{id}` · `DELETE /memories/{id}` | inspect, confirm or reject suggestions, correct, forget. All in code, no model involved. |
| `GET /bookings` | the user's bookings, from the provider's records |
| `GET /events` (SSE) | a live push of trace events while a turn runs, plus proactive notices in Phase 7 |
| `GET /sessions/{id}/runs` | past runs' traces, so the trace pane survives a reload |
| `POST /demo/...` | only when `ERRAND_DEMO_MODE=true`: next booking times out after succeeding, slot taken, provider cancels a booking, price changes at hold |

Plus the deferred **per-session lock**, so two messages in one session can't run at once (the second waits).

##### Frontend (React 19, Tailwind v4, shadcn; already scaffolded)
- **Chat:** message list with code-drawn cards:
  - **ApprovalCard:** provider · slot · price, with [Approve] [Decline]
  - **SuggestionsCard:** each suggestion with [Confirm] [Reject]
  - **BookingCard**, plus a notice style for proactive messages
  - A composer that shows "working…" while a turn runs
- **Side panel tabs:**
  - **Trace:** runs grouped and collapsible. The header shows outcome, calls, tokens, cost and time. Events are one line each with an icon and the key fact; click to see the sanitized JSON.
  - **Memory:** saved facts labelled "you told me" / "inferred", with edit and forget, plus suggestions with Confirm/Reject.
  - **Bookings:** past and upcoming.
  - **Demo:** failure triggers (dev-only).
- **Header:** backend status and **New session**.
- **Tests:** Vitest plus Testing Library, set up first (it was planned for the start of this phase).

##### Commit order (each one green)
1. Sessions + messages endpoints + per-session lock
2. Approval decision endpoint (`handler.decide`, the button path)
3. Memory + bookings endpoints
4. SSE event stream + past-run loading
5. Demo endpoints (with small `MockControl` additions), gated by setting
6. Frontend: Vitest, API client, layout shell with tabs
7. Chat + cards
8. Trace pane
9. Memory + Bookings tabs
10. Demo tab, then a manual end-to-end run with `make dev`, plus docs

**Two details to flag:**
- **Correcting a memory from the UI.** The model's `correct_memory` tool requires the user's own words as proof. In the UI, the edit form *is* the user's words, so the API passes the typed value as both the quote and the message. Provenance stays honest.
- **No word-by-word streaming of the model's reply.** The turn returns when it's done, and the live trace events show progress meanwhile. Streaming text is possible later, but it complicates the "code-written facts" guarantee.

Does this match your mental model?

**You** · 11:09 UTC

are we not using websockets for messages. Also chat messages i.e. replies from llm need to be fast so streaming messages maybe something we can look into.

**Claude**

Both are good questions. They're related, since streaming is the main reason to care about the transport.

#### 1. Streaming the replies: yes, let's do it
**Where your turn's time goes today:** your plumber turns took 5–13 s. Most of that is *several* model calls in a row (think → call a tool → read the result → answer). Only the **last** call produces text you see. So streaming helps in two ways:
- **Text appears as it's written**, from the first word instead of after the last one.
- **Progress shows during the tool phases.** The trace events we already emit become friendly status lines ("Searching plumbers in HSR Layout…", "Checking reviews…"), so the 5–10 s before the answer doesn't feel dead.

**It fits our loop.** In PydanticAI, each model-request step inside `agent.iter()` can be streamed (`node.stream(...)`) to get text as it arrives. The context guard, turn budget and tracing all run *before* each request, so they're unaffected.

**What stays code-only:** approval cards, booking confirmations ("Booked FlowRight… Booking ID…") and suggestions are still drawn by code from verified data, after the text. A typed "yes" doesn't use the model at all, so it's instant. One caveat: streamed text can't be taken back. If a turn later hits a limit, you'll have seen partial text, followed by the honest code-written limit message. That's acceptable, but worth knowing.

**Other speed levers, for later:**
- **Prompt caching:** our ~3,000-token tool definitions and system prompt are identical on every call. Anthropic can cache them, which mainly cuts time-to-first-word and cost.
- **Fewer model round trips per turn:** for example, the model sometimes calls `list_memories` when STATE already has what it needs.

#### 2. WebSockets or SSE?
Either can carry streamed text. The difference is how messages travel:

| | **SSE** (HTTP streaming) | **WebSocket** |
|---|---|---|
| Sending a message | `POST /messages`; the **response is a stream** of events: progress, text deltas, then the final reply with cards | send `{type: "message"}` over the open socket |
| Push without a request (Phase 7: "your provider cancelled") | a separate `GET /events` stream | the same socket |
| Stopping a running turn | abort the request; the server cancels the turn | send `{type: "stop"}` |
| Reconnects | built into the browser's `EventSource`, and each turn is its own request | we write reconnect and resend logic |
| Testing and debugging | plain HTTP: `curl`, FastAPI's test client, readable in dev tools | needs a socket client; message protocol to design |
| Used by | the OpenAI and Anthropic APIs themselves, the Vercel AI SDK | chat apps with typing indicators, presence, many users |

**My recommendation is SSE.** It's how the model providers stream to us, and it's the common pattern for LLM chat UIs. Each turn stays an ordinary request, which suits our per-session lock and makes tests simple. We don't need what WebSockets add: there's one user, no typing indicators and no presence. A Stop button works fine with SSE.

WebSockets would be the better fit if this became multi-user and real-time. Even then, swapping later only touches the transport layer: the event types (progress, delta, card, final, notice) stay the same.

**The plan changes to:**
- `POST /sessions/{id}/messages` returns an **event stream**: `progress` → `delta`… → `final` (outcome plus cards), with `trace` events interleaved for the trace pane.
- `GET /events` stays for unsolicited pushes (Phase 7).
- The chat renders deltas as they arrive and adds a **Stop** button.
- **One new backend commit** comes first: streaming support in `run_agent`, `on_text` and `on_progress` callbacks, tested offline with the scripted model.

Do you agree with SSE plus streaming, or would you prefer WebSockets?

- `POST /api/sessions/{id}/messages` answers with an **SSE stream**. Events arrive in this order:
  - `tool`: a tool is about to run
  - `text`: reply chunks as they're written
  - `trace`: the session's trace events, live
  - `final`: the authoritative reply with its cards
- One turn at a time per session.
- Without an API key, the app still starts; a turn then returns 503 saying what to set.

**Next: the approval decision endpoint** (the **button** path), `POST /api/approvals/{id}/decision` `{approve, fingerprint}`. One design point:
- **The UI sends back the fingerprint of the card it displayed.** So if the details changed after the card was drawn (a new price, say), the click is refused with "this changed, here are the new details" instead of approving something you didn't see. That's the assignment's "approval of the exact action", enforced on the button path just as on the typed one.
- It reuses the handler's existing confirm and decline code (as a new `handler.decide(...)`), so typed and clicked decisions take one path. The trace records `via="button"` instead of `"typed"`.

**Claude**

Starting the button path. The behaviour the tests will pin:
- **Approve, with the fingerprint of the card shown** → granted `via="button"`, then the same execution as a typed yes, so you get "Booked…".
- **Fingerprint doesn't match** (the details changed after the card was drawn) → nothing is booked. The reply says the details changed and carries the **current** card.
- **Decline** → declines **this** card only, unlike a typed "no", which declines everything pending.
- **Double click** (the card is already decided) → no second booking, and a plain explanation.
- The conversation records the click as a code-written user line, `[Approved on the card: FlowRight Plumbers, Sat 07 Nov 11:00, ₹1200]`, so the model's next turn knows what you decided.

- `POST /api/memories/{id}/confirm` and `/reject`: suggestions.
- `PATCH /api/memories/{id}` `{value}`: correct a fact. The store requires the user's own words as proof, and a form field you typed in counts as your words, so the API passes it as both the quote and the message.
- `DELETE /api/memories/{id}`: forget. This also scrubs the fact from the stored conversation, as the existing store already does.
- `GET /api/bookings`: past and upcoming, from the provider's records.

The 422 case needed one detail: pydantic's validation error is a kind of `ValueError`, so it has to be caught first or it would come back as a misleading 409.

**Next: the live stream and past runs for the trace pane.**
- `GET /api/events?session_id=…`: an SSE stream of every trace event for that session as it happens, including runs nobody typed (the startup reconcile pass, and Phase 7's "your provider cancelled"). It sends a keep-alive every 15 s so the browser connection stays open.
- `GET /api/sessions/{id}/runs`: the session's past runs, read back from the trace files, so the trace pane is complete after a page reload.

#### Next: the frontend, proposed before building
**Stack:** the scaffold's React 19, Tailwind v4 and shadcn, plus a few shadcn components (tabs, card, badge, scroll area, textarea). **Vitest + Testing Library** for tests. No state library: plain React hooks are enough for one screen.

**How it talks to the backend:**
- **Sending a message:** the browser's built-in `EventSource` can't send a POST, so the client reads the POST response as a stream with `fetch()` and parses the SSE frames itself (a small, unit-tested parser).
- **Push stream:** `EventSource` on `/api/events`, which handles reconnection itself.

**Components:**
- `App`: header (backend status, **New session**) plus a two-column layout.
- **Chat:** message list, composer (Enter sends, Shift+Enter adds a new line), and while a turn runs, a progress line ("Searching plumbers…" from `tool` events) plus the streamed text. On `final`, the bubble shows the authoritative reply and its cards:
  - **ApprovalCard:** provider · slot · price, with Approve/Decline. It sends back the fingerprint it displayed.
  - **SuggestionsCard:** Confirm/Reject per suggestion.
  - **BookingCard:** the confirmation, with the booking id.
- **Side panel tabs:**
  - **Trace:** runs, newest first, collapsible. The header shows outcome, calls, tokens and cost. Events are one line each (icon, type, key fact); click to see the JSON.
  - **Memory:** saved facts ("you told me" / "inferred"), with edit and forget, plus suggestions.
  - **Bookings:** upcoming and past.
  - **Demo:** only when the server says demo mode is on.

**Order:**
1. Vitest, the API client and SSE parser (tested), and the layout shell with tabs
2. Chat with streaming and cards
3. Trace tab
4. Memory and Bookings tabs
5. Demo tab, a `make demo` target (`make dev` with demo mode on), and an end-to-end run in the browser

Does that match your picture? Anything you'd like to look different?

I've added the cursor fix in `frontend/src/index.css`, and dark mode is a proposal below, waiting for your OK.

**Cursor:** buttons, tabs, dropdowns and expandable rows now show the hand cursor. Disabled native controls (the dropdown and the % field) show "not allowed". Disabled buttons get the plain arrow instead, because the UI kit turns off mouse events on them. Nothing is committed, as you asked.

**Dark mode proposal (no code yet)**

The dark colours already exist in `src/index.css` from the UI kit's setup; nothing switches them on yet. The only fixed colour in our own code is the green "connected" dot in the header, and that works on both backgrounds. So most of the work is the switch itself:

1. **A small theme hook** (`src/hooks/useTheme.ts`):
   - On first visit it follows your computer's light/dark setting.
   - Once you pick one, it remembers your choice in the browser.
   - It applies the theme by adding or removing the `dark` class on `<html>`.
2. **A sun/moon button in the header** (`src/components/ThemeToggle.tsx`), next to the session controls. One click flips between light and dark.
3. **No white flash on reload:** a few lines in `index.html` apply your saved theme before the page first draws. Otherwise a dark-mode user would see light flash for a moment first.
4. **Tests first:** clicking the button puts `dark` on `<html>` and remembers it; a later load picks the saved choice; with nothing saved, it follows the computer's setting.
5. **Docs:** a line in `docs/ARCHITECTURE.md`.

Files touched: `useTheme.ts`, `ThemeToggle.tsx` and its test (all new), plus `App.tsx`, `index.html` and `ARCHITECTURE.md`.

One choice for you: a two-way light/dark switch, or three-way light / dark / follow my computer? I'd go two-way, since the first visit already follows your computer, but three-way lets you go back to following it later.

Before proposing the resize, I'll check the current state of the usual resize library; its API changed recently:

Neither is hard. Here's the plan for both.

**1. Catppuccin dark** (CSS only, one file)

Today's dark background is near-black (about `#0a0a0a`). I'd replace the colour values in the `.dark` block of `frontend/src/index.css` with Catppuccin's palette. Nothing else changes, because every component already reads its colours from that block.

| Role | Catppuccin colour |
|---|---|
| Page background | base |
| Cards, popovers | mantle / surface0 |
| Muted areas, secondary buttons | surface0 |
| Borders | surface1 |
| Main text | text |
| Secondary text | subtext0 |
| Primary buttons, focus ring | mauve / lavender (the signature Catppuccin accent) |
| Errors | red |

Catppuccin has three dark flavours: **Mocha** `#1e1e2e` (the most popular, and already much lighter than today), **Macchiato** `#24273a`, and **Frappé** `#303446` (the lightest). I'd start with Mocha; swapping to another flavour later only means changing the colour values. Light mode stays as it is unless you want Catppuccin Latte there too.

**2. Resizable side panel, collapsible to a full-width chat**
- **A drag handle between chat and panel**, using shadcn's `resizable` component. It wraps `react-resizable-panels` (v4.18, actively maintained) and gives keyboard resizing (arrow keys on the handle), minimum widths and collapsing for free. Writing this by hand would mean pointer tracking plus accessibility work for no gain.
- **A "hide panel" icon button in the header**, next to the theme toggle, so it's always in the same place (like an editor's sidebar toggle). The chat then takes the full width, and clicking again brings the panel back at its previous width. Dragging the handle all the way to the edge collapses it too.
- **Width and hidden state are remembered** across reloads.
- **Narrow screens:** the side-by-side resizable layout applies everywhere. Phones aren't a target for this demo, so I'd drop the current stacked layout for small screens rather than maintain two layouts.
- **Tests:** the hide button removes the side panel and gives the chat the full width; clicking again restores it. Dragging can't be tested in jsdom (it has no layout), so I'll check that by hand, and you'll see it live.
- **Files:**
  - new: `ui/resizable.tsx` (generated by shadcn) and a Workspace test
  - edited: `Workspace.tsx`, `App.tsx` (the button; the hidden state moves up to App), `package.json`, `index.css`, `ARCHITECTURE.md`

Mocha as the starting flavour? And does this approach align with your mental model?

Back to your request. There's one more thing to mention before the plan: the "Save / Dismiss" buttons on the suggestions card in the chat (`chat/cards.tsx`) have no error handling. If the request fails, nothing happens on screen, and the error only reaches the browser console as an unhandled promise rejection. Toasts fix that naturally.

**1. Toasts for changes**

I'd use shadcn's toast component (`sonner`, the standard one). It draws its colours from the same theme values as everything else, so it automatically matches light mode and Catppuccin dark. One toaster sits in the app, bottom-right.

| Action | Success toast | On error |
|---|---|---|
| Memory tab: confirm / reject suggestion, forget | "Saved: …" / "Dismissed" / "Forgotten: …" | error toast (replaces the small red line under the row) |
| Memory tab: edit | "Updated: …" | **stays inline** under the field; "budget must be a number" belongs next to what you're typing |
| Chat suggestions card: Save / Dismiss | toast | error toast (fixes the silent failure) |
| Demo tab actions | toast with what it set (replaces the status line) | error toast |
| New session | "Started a new session" | error toast |
| Approve / Decline on a booking card | **none**: the outcome already arrives as a chat message, so a toast would repeat it | unchanged (shown as a chat notice) |

**2. Clearer selected states**
- **Side panel tabs:** in dark mode the active tab is barely tinted today (a 30%-transparent fill). The active tab would get a clearly lighter solid background, full-strength text, and an accent-coloured bar underneath (mauve in dark, near-black in light). Inactive tabs stay muted.
- **Other selections of the same kind:** the expandable rows in the Trace tab (a run, and an event inside it). Today the only sign that a row is open is the chevron. An open row would get a tinted background and an accent bar on its left, so you can see what's expanded while scrolling.
  - Those row buttons would also tell screen readers whether they're open (`aria-expanded`), and the styling would key off that same attribute, so the tests can check it.
- I checked the rest: the Demo dropdowns are native controls, and hover and focus states already show clearly.

**Tests first:**
- A toast appears after confirming a suggestion, after a demo action and after a failed card save.
- An expanded trace row reports `aria-expanded="true"`.
- The tab colours are pure styling, so you'll judge those by eye.

The server sends `created_at` via `isoformat()`. If that datetime has no timezone attached, the browser would read it as local time, and IST times would be off by 5½ hours. I'll check:

I agree it looks off. With your messages boxed and the agent's text loose on the page, the agent's reply reads like page content rather than half of a conversation. Unboxed replies suit ChatGPT- or Claude-style apps, where answers are long documents. Ours are short replies, so a bubble on both sides fits better.

I checked the timestamps. The server stores every message with its UTC offset (`…+00:00`), so the browser converts it to your local time correctly, with no IST/UTC mix-up.

**Plan**

1. **Agent bubble:** left-aligned, the same rounded shape as yours, in a quiet surface colour (Catppuccin's surface0 in dark, light grey in light) so your messages stay the accented ones.
   - The approval, booking and suggestion cards render **below** the bubble, not inside it. Cards inside a bubble would be boxes within boxes and look heavy.
   - Small grey system lines, such as "Stopped showing this turn…" or a card click, stay unboxed.
2. **Times and dates:**
   - **Time** in small muted text under each bubble ("14:32"), on the same side as the bubble. Hovering shows the full date and time.
   - **Date** as a divider whenever the day changes ("Today", "Yesterday", "Thu, 5 Nov"), as in WhatsApp or Slack. I think that reads better than repeating the date on every message.
   - **History** uses the time the server stored. **New messages** take the browser clock when sent or received. After a reload the server's stored times take over; they differ by a second or so.
   - A reply that's still streaming gets its time when it finishes.
3. **Tests first:**
   - a history message shows its local time
   - a day divider appears between messages from different days, with the right label
   - the agent's reply sits in its own bubble, with its cards after it
   - The tests will run with a fixed time zone, so times are checked exactly on any machine.

**Files:**
- `chat/Chat.tsx`: bubble, time line, day dividers
- `hooks/useChat.ts`: each message carries its time
- `chat/format.ts`: time and day-label helpers
- `Chat.test.tsx`
- the Vitest config (fixed time zone)
- `ARCHITECTURE.md`

No backend change is needed.

ok now [Image #9] why is just a hello response taking 8.6 seconds. What is going on is the model thinking alos I don't think we are really streaming the response since I am gettting the full message. Is the model thinking or something?? if it is, then it shouldn't for things like this

**Claude**

I'll gather the facts before guessing: which model and settings the agent uses, and what the trace recorded for that run.

- `Bash` Check the default model and any thinking settings

No thinking settings appear anywhere in our code, and the model is Sonnet 5.5. Next, the trace recorded for that exact run (token split, timing):

- `Bash` Read that run's model call from the trace file
- `Bash` Check for middleware, the dev proxy and how the loop streams

- `Bash` Read the text-chunk filter and the SSE response setup

I've ruled two things out. Here's what we know, then my hypotheses.

**What we know (from the trace and the code)**
- **Not thinking:** one model call, 3,383 tokens in, **52 tokens out**. Anthropic counts thinking as output tokens, so if the model had thought, that number would be in the hundreds. Our code also never turns thinking on (`claude-sonnet-5-5` with default settings).
- **The time is inside that one call:** the trace times the model request itself (`latency_ms: 8596`). Our own code before and after it adds about 20 ms.
- **The server is set up to stream:** the agent loop forwards each piece of text as it arrives (`loop.py:139-143`), and the response is a streamed event feed. Nothing in our code waits for the full reply. That's in theory, though: when I tested earlier I counted 29 text chunks, but I **didn't time them**, so they could have arrived all at once.

**Three hypotheses**

| | Explains the 8.6 s | Explains "full message at once" | What would show it |
|---|---|---|---|
| **H1: the API is slow to start replying** (it was busy, or the SDK quietly retried after an "overloaded" or rate-limit error, with back-off) | ✅ | ✅ (the 52 tokens then stream out in under a second, which looks instant) | the first text chunk arrives late, but the chunks are spread out after it |
| **H2: the dev proxy holds the stream back** (Vite's `/api` proxy forwarding the event feed in one piece) | ❌ (the 8.6 s was measured on the server) | ✅ | chunks spread out when hitting `:8000` directly, bunched together via `:5173` |
| **H3: the model library gives us the reply in one piece** (PydanticAI's streaming for this model handing over the text at the end) | partly | ✅ | chunks bunched together even at `:8000` |

H1 alone could explain both symptoms. A 52-token reply finishes generating in well under a second, so "first chunk at 8 s, last chunk at 8.4 s" would look exactly like the whole message appearing at once.

**Two checks that separate them**
1. **Yours, free, in the browser:** open DevTools → Network, send "hello", click the `messages` POST and open its **EventStream** tab. Each `text` event has a time. Are they all in the same moment, or spread over a few hundred milliseconds? And how long after the request does the first one arrive?
2. **Mine, about $0.03, with your OK:** I'd measure three times on the same prompt:
   - first and last chunk calling Anthropic directly with no code of ours involved (tests H1)
   - the same from our server on `:8000` (H3)
   - the same through the dev proxy on `:5173` (H2)

   That's three short Sonnet calls of about 3.4K input tokens each, using your API key from `backend/.env`.

What does the EventStream tab show? And shall I run the three timings?

**Claude**

Your paste answers part of it. Two quick checks so the plan is concrete: does our installed PydanticAI support Anthropic prompt caching, and what's the minimum prompt length that can be cached?

- `Bash` Check PydanticAI cache settings and cache minimums
- `Bash` Read which model has always-on thinking and the cache minimums
- `Bash` Read the Sonnet 5.5 thinking and effort notes

I have what I need. One correction first, then the plan.

**Correction.** I told you the model wasn't thinking because "our code never turns thinking on". That reasoning was wrong: **Sonnet 5.5 thinks by default** (adaptive, at `high` effort, with the thinking text hidden), and it can't be fully switched off. For "hello" the numbers still show it barely thought: 52–53 output tokens is just the reply, and thinking would be billed in that same count. But on real turns (searching, booking) at `high` effort, it can think a lot.

**What your paste shows**
- **Streaming works end to end:** 12 separate `text` events reached your browser through the dev proxy. So it's neither H2 (the proxy holding the stream back) nor H3 (the library delivering it in one piece).
- **It looks instant anyway:** a ~50-token reply is generated in about half a second, so the chunks land in a burst. Nearly all the wait is **before the first word**. Here that was 3.5 s; the run before was 8.6 s for the same prompt, which points to H1, the API itself being slower at some moments.
- **The real UI bug:** for a turn with no tool call, **nothing appears** between sending and the first word. The progress line only shows when a tool runs, so the UI is silent for those seconds.

**Plan**

*A. UI (no cost)*
1. **Typing dots:** three animated dots in an agent bubble from the moment you send until the first word. A tool's progress line replaces them when one runs.
2. **Caret while streaming:** a blinking caret at the end of the reply until the final arrives.
3. **Smooth reveal:** chunks arrive in bursts, so the text is revealed at a steady pace instead. It never trails more than about half a second behind the real text, so it can't make replies slower.

*B. Model (to make the wait itself shorter)*

PydanticAI 2.66 supports both settings below (`anthropic_effort`, `anthropic_cache_tool_definitions` / `anthropic_cache_instructions`):

4. **Lower effort:** the Anthropic docs' recommendation for latency-sensitive routes is adaptive thinking at **`low`** effort, which keeps thinking short and skips it on most simple requests. This would be a setting (`ERRAND_EFFORT`, default `low`), with a decision entry in DECISIONS.md.
5. **Prompt caching:** we don't cache today. The tool definitions (2,858 tokens) and system prompt (719) are identical on every turn, and Sonnet 5.5 can cache any prompt prefix of 512 tokens or more. Repeat turns would re-read them from cache at about **0.1× the input price** and with less processing before the first word.
   - Our system prompt also carries the per-turn state and memories, which change every turn. I'll check where those sit, so the cached part stays stable.
6. **Measure, not guess:** a small `make latency` script sends 3 prompts (hello, a plumber search, a booking request) and prints time to first word and total time. I'd run it before and after 4–5, about **$0.10** in total on your key.

The offline tests use a fake model, so they're unaffected. The risk with `low` effort is answer quality on the multi-step booking flow, which is why step 6 includes a booking prompt and I'd show you its reply.

Does this approach align with your mental model? And is the ~$0.10 for measurements OK?

Baseline done. That pass cost **$0.10**, so with the crashed attempt we've now used the ~$0.10 you approved. Here's what it shows:

| Turn | First word | Total | Model calls | Output tokens | Cost |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| hello | 1.15 s | 2.2 s | 1 | 103 | $0.008 |
| hello | 0.90 s | 1.7 s | 1 | 53 | $0.007 |
| hello | 1.07 s | 2.2 s | 1 | 100 | $0.008 |
| Find me a plumber… | **13.4 s** | 13.5 s | **6** | 1,780 | $0.044 |
| Book the cheapest… | 2.2 s | 10.1 s | 4 | 749 | $0.035 |

**What this tells us**
1. **"hello" is ~1 s to first word right now.** Your 3.5 s and 8.6 s came from the same single model call with the same prompt, so the API simply varies. We can't control that, but faster calls give it less to stretch.
2. **The model does think on "hello", sometimes.** The same ~50-token reply came with 103 and 100 output tokens in two runs and 53 in the third, so about 50 hidden thinking tokens. That's small here, but it shows `high` effort thinks even on trivial turns.
3. **The real wait is the errand turns.** The plumber search took **13.4 s to its first word, over 6 model calls**: the model calls tools one after another, and no text appears until the last call. 1,780 output tokens over 6 calls means a lot of thinking between tool calls.
4. **Caching would help errand turns most.** Those 6 calls each resent the same ~3.6K-token prefix (19,381 input tokens in total), with nothing cached.

The booking quality is right: the reply ends at the approval card ("It isn't booked yet").

**Next, needs your OK (~$0.20 more):** two more passes, about $0.10 each:
- `--effort low`, to see the effect of effort alone on time, thinking tokens and reply quality
- `--effort low --cache`, to see what caching adds on top

**BUG-10, the silent wait**
- Typing dots now appear until the first word, and a tool's progress line replaces them when one runs.
- While the reply streams, a blinking caret sits at the end, and the text appears at a steady pace instead of in one burst.

**The wait itself (D5 in DECISIONS.md)**

I measured with `make latency`, which runs the real agent on throwaway data (`a7e2c7e`, then `bb1b082`):

| | Before | Now |
|---|---|---|
| Plumber search, time to first word | 13.4 s, 6 model calls | about 6.5 s, 3 calls |
| Booking request, total | 10.1 s | about 6–7 s |
| A repeated "hello", cost | $0.0078 | $0.0014 |

- **Lower effort** is the speed win. Sonnet 5.5 had been thinking at its default `high` level on every turn, including between tool calls; at `low` it needs about half the model calls on errands.
- **Prompt caching** is the cost win. About 98% of a repeat call's input now comes from the cache. It didn't make replies measurably faster at our prompt size.
- **Behaviour stayed the same:** the search took the same steps, and the booking still stopped at the approval card.
- **"hello" was always about 1 s to its first word.** Your 3.5 s and 8.6 s were the API being slower at those moments, which we can't control.
- **Both settings can be turned back:** `ERRAND_EFFORT=high`, `ERRAND_PROMPT_CACHE=false`.
- **Limits:** each setting was measured once, on one day, and on two kinds of errand turn. That's enough to see the halving, not a precise worst case. D5 records this, and the live test run planned for Phase 8 will cover more flows.
- **Also changed:** trace costs now price cached tokens at their real rate, and the Trace tab shows "N cached" on each model call.

**Two mistakes on my side:**
- **The Makefile edit:** I added `latency` to its `.PHONY` line with a shell command instead of a reviewable edit. It's a one-word change and it's in commit `a7e2c7e`.
- **The thinking claim:** I first said the model wasn't thinking because our code never turns it on. That was wrong: Sonnet 5.5 thinks by default. I've corrected it here and in D5.

I have enough for a proposal. No code yet.

**What the assignment asks (restated)**

When a provider cancels a booking (simulated), the app must:
1. **Detect it with no user prompt.**
2. **Decide whether and how to tell the user.**
3. **Find a replacement within the user's known preferences.**
4. **Get fresh approval** before rebooking.

There must also be an eval for this ("proactive cancellation").

**Facts that shape the design**
- **What we can see:** the agent only sees the provider through the fixed tools. `list_bookings` shows a booking's **status** (`cancelled_by_provider`) but **not the reason**; the reason stays inside the mock. So the notice can say what happened, not why.
- **Which conversation:** bookings the agent made are tied to the session where they were made. Seeded ones, like the CoolBreeze booking, aren't tied to any session, so their notice goes to your most recent session.
- **Pieces already in place:** a background loop (the reconciler, every 30 s), the per-session lock, the `/api/events` push stream, approval cards enforced in code, and the "Provider cancels" demo button.

**Plan**

*1. Detect (code, no model).* A new `ProviderMonitor` in `workflow/monitor.py`:
- **Polling:** it checks `list_bookings` every 10 s (configurable) for each user who has upcoming bookings.
- **What counts:** an upcoming booking now marked `cancelled_by_provider` that we haven't handled yet.
- **Once only:** a new `provider_notices` table records each booking as handled *before* anything is sent. A cancellation is acted on exactly once, even across restarts or two overlapping checks.
- **Trace:** each detection starts its own run with `trigger="provider_cancelled"`.

*2. Decide (code rules, each traced with its reason).*

| Situation | Decision |
|---|---|
| The cancelled slot is already in the past | record only, no notice |
| You already have another active booking for that service near that time | tell you, no replacement search |
| You cancelled it yourself (`cancelled`, not `cancelled_by_provider`) | nothing; not a provider event |
| Otherwise | tell you **and** look for a replacement |

**How you're told:** the notice is always saved in the conversation. If that chat is open in a browser, it also appears live, with a toast. If not, you see it next time you open the chat.

*3. Find a replacement (the agent, i.e. the model).* The code writes the notice: "CoolBreeze AC Care cancelled your AC servicing on Wed 11 Nov, 10:00. Looking for a replacement…". Then it starts an agent run on that event, rather than on a user message.
- **What it searches for:** the same service, near the original time, within your saved preferences (time window, budget, avoided providers, preferred provider first). Those preferences are already in its context.
- **What it ends with:** it uses the same search and propose tools as a normal turn, so it ends at an **approval card**. Approval stays enforced in code: nothing is booked until you approve, exactly as today.
- **If it finds nothing in your preferences:** it says so and asks whether to widen the search.
- **If the run fails or hits a limit:** a code-written fallback still tells you, so you're never left in silence.
- **No clash with your typing:** the run takes the session's lock, so it waits for any turn you're in the middle of.

*4. See it in the UI.*
- **Live:** `/api/events` gains a `message` event. The chat appends the agent's notice and its approval card live, with a toast.
- **After a reload:** loading the chat history now also returns a **pending approval card**. Today a card disappears after a reload, which breaks the case where it was created while you were away. I found this gap while planning this phase.

*5. Steps (each one commit, tests first):*
1. Detection, decision and once-only (offline tests: detected once; your own cancellations and past slots ignored; nothing repeated after a restart).
2. The replacement run on the event (scripted-model test: it proposes a replacement within preferences and never books without approval).
3. The pending card returned with history, and live `message` push (API tests).
4. Frontend: live notices and cards, plus the pending card after a reload.
5. Docs: D6 in DECISIONS.md, ARCHITECTURE.md, TASKS.md.

**Files:**
- new: `workflow/monitor.py`, migration `0010_provider_notices.sql`
- backend edits: `agent/handler.py` (a run started by an event), `agent/prompts.py` (the event's instructions), `api/app.py` (start the monitor), `api/chat_routes.py` (pending card with history), `api/events.py` (`message` events), `core/config.py`
- frontend edits: `client.ts`, `useChat.ts`, `Chat.tsx`, and their tests

**Cost:** offline tests cost nothing. Each real cancellation in the demo triggers one agent run, about $0.01–0.03 with caching.

Two choices for you:
1. **Replacement by the agent (model) or by code?** I recommend **the agent**, as above. The assignment wants a real model driving the tool calls, and this reuses everything that already works for normal turns. Detection, the decision and approval stay in code, where they can be tested exactly. Doing it in code would be cheaper and more predictable, but it makes the "agent" in this flow just a script.
2. **Are the decision rules in the table the ones you want?** One possible extra rule: "slot less than 2 hours away: notify only, no search".

Does this approach align with your mental model?

**You** · 13:28 UTC

This will be mostly mock from backend side I am guessing right??

**Claude**

Only the provider side is a mock: the cancellation itself, and the world it happens in. That's true of the whole project, since the assignment makes all 8 provider tools mocks. Everything that reacts to the cancellation is real code.

| Part | Mock or real |
|---|---|
| The provider cancelling (the Demo tab's "Provider cancels" button, or `MockControl` in tests) | **mock**: the "simulated event" the assignment asks for |
| The provider's records that `list_bookings` reads | **mock** (the mock world's database) |
| Detecting it: polling `list_bookings`, spotting `cancelled_by_provider`, recording it once | **real**, ours |
| Deciding whether and how to tell you (the rules table) | **real**, ours |
| The notice, live push, toast, and the card after a reload | **real**, ours |
| Finding a replacement | **real model** in the app (a scripted model in offline tests) calling the same tools |
| Approval before rebooking, and the rebooking itself | **real** approval code, booking against the mock provider |

One thing I'm careful about: the monitor learns of a cancellation **only through `list_bookings`**, the same fixed tool a real provider would offer. It never reads the mock's internal event table, even though that table has the reason. A real provider wouldn't give us its database, so peeking at it would be a hidden shortcut that makes the feature look like it works when it wouldn't. As a result, you could swap in a real provider and the monitor wouldn't change.

The one part a real deployment might do differently is detection. A real provider would more likely send a webhook than be polled; if one existed, it would replace only the polling loop. Everything after detection would stay the same.

The two choices from my proposal still stand:
1. Should the agent (model) find the replacement, as I recommend, or code?
2. Are the decision rules right? And do you want the extra rule: a slot less than 2 hours away gets a notice only, no search?

Phase 7 (proactive follow-up after a provider cancels) is built on `feat/proactive` in five commits, not yet merged. All offline tests pass (720 backend, 69 frontend), and lint and types are clean. It hasn't been run with the real model yet; that's the browser check at the end.

**What happens when a provider cancels**
1. **Noticed without a prompt:** the app checks your bookings every 10 s, using only the `list_bookings` tool a real provider would offer (`29802de`).
2. **Handled once:** recording the cancellation and saving the notice happen together, so it never repeats, even after a restart.
3. **Decided by fixed rules** in code, with the reason recorded in the trace:
   - slot already passed → record only, nobody told
   - you already have another booking for that service within a day → tell you, no search
   - your own cancellation → ignored
   - otherwise → tell you and look for a replacement
4. **Replacement found by the agent:** the model searches within your saved preferences and stops at a fresh approval card. Nothing is rebooked until you say yes or click (`a82753c`). The event text can't be mistaken for your words, so the agent can't save a preference from it.
5. **Shown in the chat:**
   - The notice arrives live with a toast, then the replacement card (`1beccf5`, `98b3343`).
   - The replacement waits for any turn you're in the middle of.
   - With no model configured, you get a message saying so instead of silence.
   - A card made while the chat was closed shows up when you reopen it.
6. **Documented:** D6 in DECISIONS.md, plus ARCHITECTURE.md and TASKS.md (`4813e04`).

**Two weak tests along the way, now fixed:**
- **Turn-lock test:** I broke the code on purpose to check each new test fails without its fix. The "replacement waits for your turn" test still passed with the lock removed.
- **No-duplicates test:** same result, so it proved nothing either.
- **The fixes:** both were timing-based; I rewrote them so they don't depend on timing, and now each fails when its fix is removed.
- **The provenance test:** it was also broken at first, but for a different reason. Its scripted call was invalid, so the provenance rule never ran.

**Try it** with `make demo`, then open the app:
1. Demo tab → **Provider cancels** → pick the CoolBreeze booking.
2. Within about 10 s, the notice should appear in the chat with a toast. The replacement card follows a few seconds later, and the Trace tab shows a `provider_cancelled` run, then a `provider_replacement` run.
3. Approve the card to rebook.

Each cancellation costs one agent run, about $0.01–0.03.

Once it looks right, shall I merge `feat/proactive` into main and push?

**You** · 13:55 UTC

ok how do I test this though?? will work in demo?

**Claude**

Yes, it should work in demo mode. I'll check one thing that could make the demo fizzle before giving you the steps: the agent searches in your default address's area (Home, HSR Layout). If no mock AC provider serves HSR Layout, it would just say "nothing fits".

- `Bash` Check mock AC providers serve the user's areas

Yes, it works in demo mode, and the mock has three AC providers serving HSR Layout with plenty of slots, so the agent has something to propose. Here's how to test it.

**Setup**
1. **Restart `make demo` and reload the page.** The reload server might pick up the new code by itself, but the new database table and the monitor loop are set up at startup, so a clean restart is safest.
2. **Have your newest session open.** The seeded CoolBreeze booking wasn't made by the agent, so its notice goes to your latest session. "New session" gives you one.

**The main test**
1. Demo tab → **Provider cancels** → pick **CoolBreeze AC Care** → click.
2. **Within ~10 s:** a toast and a chat message saying "CoolBreeze AC Care cancelled your AC servicing booking for … I'm looking for a replacement…".
   - The Bookings tab shows it as *cancelled by provider*.
   - The Trace tab shows a `provider_cancelled` run with a 📅 cancellation line and a 🔔 `replace · …` decision.
3. **~5–10 s later:** the agent's reply with an approval card for a replacement, and a `provider_replacement` run in the Trace tab. Between the notice and the card there are **no typing dots**, because this run isn't streamed to the browser; the reply arrives whole. The notice's "I'm looking…" covers the gap. I can add dots there if you want.
4. **Approve:** it books, and the new booking appears in the Bookings tab.

That costs one agent run, about $0.01–0.03.

**Other cases worth a try**
- **Chat closed:** cancel, close the tab right away, wait ~15 s, reopen. The notice and the card should be in the history, with the card still approvable.
- **Restart:** cancel, then stop the server within a few seconds and start it again. You should get exactly one notice.
- **A booking the agent made** (e.g. your FlowRight plumber): its notice goes to the session it was booked in. If that isn't the session you have open, you won't see it live; that's expected, not a bug.
- **Repeating the test:** each booking can only be cancelled by the provider once. The replacement you just approved is a new upcoming booking, so you can cancel that one next.
- **"Notify only":** hard to set up from the UI (it needs two bookings for the same service within a day). The offline tests cover it.

**One thing to watch:** the agent might propose **CoolBreeze again**, at another time or even the same freed slot. Nothing tells it to avoid the provider that just cancelled. If that feels wrong when you see it, I can add "prefer a different provider" to the instructions, or make it a rule in code.

If something doesn't show up, the run's trace file in `backend/var/traces/` and the server log will tell us where it stopped.
